On a GB10 with Nemotron 3.5 Lightning (MLX 4-bit), a new chat's ~1,000-token first turn sent right after another reply started after 0.199 s, as in 1.0.8 (0.200 s) where the first merge of the background pass took 0.240 s, and longer ones kept #720's gain: 6% sooner than 1.0.8 at ~10k tokens and 7% at ~20k. In chats with a second between turns, later turns resumed past each reply and started 2.0-2.2x sooner than in 1.0.8.
- Nemotron on CUDA times the draft-depth rule's window costs on the window a drafted round verifies: the pending token at the prompt's end, the MTP head drafting first as in a round, then the text's own next tokens as the window's drafts. 1.0.8 advanced a token before timing. The window's drafts are never the head's own chain, which loops past its confident levels (" the river bank, the river was still the same river"): rows that repeat a token share experts, and such windows read 7-10% under what decoding pays at 8 to 11 rows. Replies are unchanged. On a GB10 the table's shape is within 2.8% of what decoding pays, and over 16 prompts the mean decode speed is within 0.4% of 1.0.8's table for code and prose, greedy and sampled.
- `/v1/responses` accepts OpenAI's `include` values (such as `reasoning.encrypted_content` or `message.output_text.logprobs`) and ignores them, where it used to refuse any `include`, so clients that always send it can call the server. A value that is not one of OpenAI's names is still refused (#759). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- GLM tool calls that the model wrote slightly wrong still parse: an argument key written without its `<arg_key>` gets it back, and a call the end token cut off inside its value is closed when the closed call then parses strictly. A key with no value, a tool not offered, or text after an argument still stays as reply text. The repairs and their cases come from @MiaAI-Lab's #285 (#761). Thanks to @Bizuayeu and @MiaAI-Lab.
- The native server's tokenizer keeps the ids of long text pieces between added tokens (up to 64 MiB), so a chat's earlier messages are not tokenized again: each request renders its chat up to three times, and every turn sends the earlier messages back. Encoding gives the same ids. On a GB10 with Nemotron, the server's time before the prompt pass on a later turn fell from 12.6 to 1.6 ms at 12k tokens and from 41.5 to 2.3 ms at 35k, with every reply the same. Every family the native server serves goes through this tokenizer. On a GB10 and an M3 Ultra, `/tokenize` gives the same ids as the build before it for every turn of two five-turn chats and for three long documents (Nemotron on both, Qwen3.8-27B on the Mac), and the tokenizers of Nemotron, Qwen3.8-27B, Qwen3.5-2B, Flash Next and GLM-5.3 give a fresh encode's ids through the memo; the tokenizer's own tests now run in `test-cuda-host` (#767). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA drafts every stream of a shared round in one batch: each head level runs one forward with a row per stream still drafting, where each stream's head used to run its levels one row at a time. Drafts and their confidences equal the one-at-a-time ones to the bit, greedy and sampled, so replies are unchanged. On a GB10 with distinct prompts, 400-token replies finish 5% sooner at 2 streams, 7% at 4 and 10% at 8 (315 against 282 tok/s); a lone stream is unchanged. A batched head runs with a captured Triton set too: it normalizes the streams' last rows in one launch on aligned rows, as that set needs. A build that drafted each batched stream again alone found no draft id or confidence that differed in 4,300 rounds and 34,460 levels (MLX 4-bit with our kernels and with the captured set, and NVFP4). Together with #769, #770 and #771, on a GB10 (MLX 4-bit, distinct prompts, 256-token replies) aggregate decode rose 4.4-5.5% at 2 streams, 9.4-11.6% at 4 and 11.8-14.7% at 8 over the build before them, every reply equal to its solo run (#768). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- A shared round prices each stream's drafts by the head steps they take, apart from the round's own overhead, and a head that drafts every stream in one batch costs the deepest stream's steps. The overhead learned before also held the steps of the depths it was measured at, so it kept the depths it saw. On a GB10 with Nemotron's batched heads and distinct prompts, 4 streams finish 3.3% to 3.5% sooner across three sessions and 2, 3 and 6 streams 0.6% to 2.6%, with the same replies; 8 streams are unchanged. A family whose head drafts one stream at a time pays a head step a draft: on an M3 Ultra, Nemotron's shared rounds at 2, 4 and 8 streams measured within 3% of the build before it either way (a mean of +0.8%) with the same replies, and the 27B, whose budgets carry no head steps, within 1% (#769). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA runs a shared round's per-stream steps for all its streams at once: each Mamba layer's conv and scan, and each attention layer's key write, chunks and merge, take one launch for every stream instead of one per stream, each stream still on its own state, caches and position. Every stream's rows equal its one-at-a-time ones to the bit, so replies are unchanged. On a GB10 with distinct prompts, 400-token replies finish 5% to 8% sooner at 8 streams and 3% at 4 across sessions; one and two streams are unchanged. `tf-cuda-test glue` compares the shared-window kernels with the one-stream ones byte for byte, for eight conv and scan shapes and eight attention shapes in every slot of eight streams and in two- and three-stream windows (#770). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA keeps more of each decode step's loads in flight: the MoE sum loads a row's eight slot values before adding them, the Mamba scan loads every step's inputs before its steps, and an attention window of several rows loads its next key tile while it computes the current one. The values and the order of every sum are unchanged, so replies are unchanged. On a GB10, one 400-token reply decodes 2% faster, and at 56k and 112k tokens of context 3% and 5% faster. On a GB10 with MLX 4-bit, a lone stream decoded 1.9% to 2.4% faster over eight prompts, greedy and sampled, on a shared window-cost table and on each build's own, and cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens prefill as before (within 0.7%). `tf-cuda-test glue` now checks one-row windows as well, which keep the plain chunk kernel (#771). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Qwen3.8-27B serves on Apple's GPU generation 18 (M6). A Mac mini M6 (12-core GPU, 32 GB) ran the 27B with its DFlash2 drafter on 1.0.8 with this one change: drafted replies equal to `"draft": false` byte for byte at 16k and 32k contexts, thinking budgets cut where asked, long-context recall, no swap, and the same reply hashes as earlier builds on that Mac. Later generations still refuse until someone runs them (#643). Thanks to @RS91Dev.
- The 27B and Flash Next on a Mac keep a prompt state inside prompts under 4,096 tokens, where they kept none: the history's mark, at the last chunk end the prompt pass cuts anyway, so it adds no prompt call. A repeated first turn now resumes, and so does a follow-up with thinking on, whose reply state never prefixes the next turn because the template drops the reasoning. On an M3 Ultra at the default cache budget, a 27B follow-up of about 2,900 tokens with thinking on starts in 0.4-0.5 s instead of 10.4-10.6 s, and a repeated 1,600-token first turn in 0.18 s instead of 5.5 s; on an M5 Ultra, Flash Next's follow-ups of 1,000-3,000 tokens start in 0.08-0.10 s instead of 0.35-0.82 s. Replies are byte for byte the same, and first turns take under 0.5% longer. A short prompt's mark inside its request's shared system block is kept as the block's state, so the request's own reply state no longer evicts it first.
- On a Mac, a Nemotron MoE layer that Living Weights taught runs its pocket as two dispatches beside other work, where it ran three after the shared expert, each behind a barrier: `tf_pocket_in` (the dot product and squared relu in one kernel, adding in the old kernel's order) runs beside the router and the shared expert's up projection, and the pocket's output beside the routed experts' down projection. Each sum keeps its order, so replies are unchanged. On an M5 Max, `tf_pocket_in` takes 4.6 to 5.6 us a call, under the 21 us of the up projection beside it. On an M3 Ultra, a folder taught two facts gave the same replies token for token, drafted and with `"draft": false`, and decoded 8.2% faster greedy and 8.3% sampled over eight prompts; the kernel test is byte-equal there too (#775). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- `prompt_logprobs` on CUDA reads its prompt rows through the head 256 rows a pass instead of 16, so the head's weights are read once for every 256 rows, into a logits buffer of its own (64 MiB, made at the first such request; without the memory it keeps 16-row passes). The rows are unchanged to the bit. On a GB10, the extra cost of `prompt_logprobs` on an 8,741-token prompt fell from 0.70 s to 0.13 s (2.03 s to 1.46 s in all at 0 alternatives, 2.53 s to 1.79 s at 20), and on a 3,377-token prompt from 0.27 s to 0.05 s. On a GB10 the rows were byte-equal to the 16-row passes at 300, 3,377, 4,097 and 8,741 tokens with 0 and 20 alternatives, on MLX 4-bit (our kernels and the captured set) and NVFP4. Over four servers a build (MLX 4-bit), the extra time at 8,741 tokens fell from 0.71 s to 0.12 s at 0 alternatives and from 1.21 s to 0.47 s at 20, and at 3,377 tokens from 0.29 s to 0.06 s. The buffer stays for the server's life once made (#776). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA decodes a lone request that asks for `logprobs` on its lone driver, as it does requests without them: the driver commits each round's kept rows as the lane core's windows read them, so the rows and tokens are unchanged. On a GB10, a 400-token greedy reply with `logprobs` costs 0.3% more than one without at 0 alternatives, 0.9% at 5 and 3.1% at 20, against 4.6%, 5.6% and 8.4% before. On a GB10 (MLX 4-bit with our kernels and with the captured set, and NVFP4), the lone driver's rows and tokens equalled the lane core's byte for byte at 0, 5 and 20 alternatives, greedy and seeded sampled, with thinking on and off, streamed and not, and equalled `"draft": false`. Over four servers a build, a 400-token reply with `logprobs` cost 0.4%, 1.4% and 4.1% more than one without, against 4.6%, 5.6% and 8.9% before (#777). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA decodes a lone request with a thinking budget above 32 tokens, or with `--loop-guard`, on its lone driver, which hands the stream to the lane core just before the budget's cut or a loop guard's close, so the close is forced as before and replies are unchanged. On a GB10, 700-token thinking replies with a 4,000-token budget finished 3.3% and 8.1% sooner, with a 400-token budget that cuts 3.6 to 4.2% sooner, and with `--loop-guard` 4% and 8% sooner. On a GB10 (MLX 4-bit and NVFP4), thinking budgets of 33, 48, 400, 1,000 and 4,000 tokens gave the lane core's replies token for token, greedy and seeded sampled, cut at the same token, and equalled `"draft": false`; the short budgets went to the lane core just before their cut and the long ones stayed on the lone driver to the end. With `--loop-guard`, prompts that make the reasoning repeat a token stopped at the same token with the same period as before (15 of 24 replies over the two formats), and every reply equalled the previous build's and `"draft": false`. Over four servers a build, 700-token thinking replies with a 4,000-token budget finished 6.3% and 8.7% sooner and one cut at 400 tokens 4.0% sooner (#778). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA decodes a lone structured-output request (`response_format`, a required tool call) on its lone driver: each window's chain is cut and grown by the grammar and its rows masked as the lane core's are, so replies are unchanged. On a GB10, 500-token JSON replies finished 8% sooner under `json_object` and 13% under a JSON schema, and required tool calls 14% sooner. On a GB10 (MLX 4-bit with both kernel sets, and NVFP4), a JSON object, two JSON schemas, a regex, a choice, required and named tool calls, and a schema and a required call after a 300-token thinking budget all gave the lane core's replies token for token, greedy and seeded sampled, and equalled `"draft": false`. Over four servers a build, a 370-token `json_object` reply finished 4% to 7% sooner and a required tool call 15% sooner (#779). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Nemotron on CUDA hands the lane core's last live stream back to its lone driver once the requests beside it finish and nothing waits: the driver resumes from the round the lane core drafted, so replies are unchanged. On a GB10, an 800-token greedy reply started beside a short one finished about 5% sooner; seeded sampled replies were unchanged. Only a lone driver that resumes a stream gets one back: on a Mac, Nemotron's and the 27B's lone drivers open every stream they are given, so a stream the lane core holds stays there to its end, as before. On a GB10 (MLX 4-bit with both kernel sets, and NVFP4), replies started beside a short one equalled their runs alone, greedy and seeded sampled, with `logprobs`, a JSON object and a thinking budget too, and 2, 4 and 8 concurrent requests equalled their solo runs; over four servers a build, an 800-token reply beside a 10-token one finished 1% to 2% sooner. On an M3 Ultra, Nemotron and the 27B kept concurrent replies equal to solo at 2 and 4 streams (#780). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- The 27B on a Mac builds its Neural Engine prompt share only when the share fits under the 70% load limit beside the loaded model. The share keeps an fp16 copy of 3,072 MLP columns of every layer, 5,760 MiB, outside the GPU's own count, and on a 32 GB Mac it took the server into swap for about 5% faster prompts (#643, reported by @RS91Dev). Such a Mac now runs prompts on the GPU alone and says so at load; with the 4-bit checkpoint, Macs with 48 GB or more keep the share.
- `--cortheon` puts Cortheon's reasoning program and web research inside the native server, for chat completions, `/v1/responses` and `/v1/messages`, with no change for clients. The model gets Cortheon's stages for its task type and two tools the server runs itself, `cortheon_search` (DuckDuckGo's HTML results, or Brave's API with `BRAVE_SEARCH_API_KEY`) and `cortheon_fetch`, whose calls the client never sees. A question about current or version-specific facts, or a request to build with an outside library or API, is researched before the model starts: the model writes up to three queries, and the server reads up to three of the best pages. A coding task that changed files after its last test is asked once to run it before finishing, and a researched answer without a URL is asked once for its sources. Web text loses sentences that read as orders to the model, and reads reach public addresses only, each redirect checked, pinned to the checked address, with TLS verified, 2 MiB and 15 seconds at most. The rounds a client never saw are kept and put back in front of their reply on the next turn, so the prompt cache resumes it. Without the flag every reply is unchanged. See [Cortheon mode](docs/cortheon.md).
- Flash Next loads in Debug and ReleaseSafe builds of the native engine. `config.pleRef` built its n-gram multipliers with `golden * (i + 1)`, which overflows 64 bits and panicked those builds at load; it now wraps, as the seed's addition beside it does. Release builds computed the same values before and after (#783). Thanks to @grantoverton.
- Nemotron and Qwen3.5-2B on a Mac refuse `--speed-up` at startup, where they took it and did nothing; the 27B's refusal now reads the same way, and Flash Next, GLM-5.3-Flash and GLM-5.3 still take it. A CUDA build refuses `--speed-up`, and the CUDA server refuses `--prompt-cache-over-cap`, and `--drafter` for a family that drafts with its own MTP head, before it loads anything, instead of ignoring them (#733). Thanks to @jschmied.

## 1.0.8

- Nemotron on CUDA keeps and resumes prompt states at any row instead of only on the 2,048-row grid, so a conversation resumes from where its last turn's history ended, and chats of 2,048 tokens or fewer resume too. Every kernel a prompt chunk runs gives a prompt row the same bits wherever the prompt is cut into chunks: dumps of every layer's rows, the final states, the MTP head's caches and the first token were byte-equal at 17 cut patterns on a GB10 and an RTX PRO 6000, with the built-in kernels and with the captured GB10 set. A state kept inside a chunk costs no extra prompt call: as the chunk's scan passes that row it stores the Mamba state there, into a buffer the cache keeps free, and the key and value rows are copied once the chunk is queued. Those states were byte-equal to the ones a chunk ending at the same rows leaves, at the same 17 patterns on both GPUs. On a GB10, turns 2 to 5 of chats from 350 to 16,000 tokens started 1.7 to 2.8 times sooner (medians 0.09 to 0.11 s, against 0.15 to 0.30 s). Cold first turns took as long as before from 10,000 tokens, and 1 to 3 ms longer (1 to 2%) under 1,400 tokens, which now keep two or three states where they kept none; on an RTX PRO 6000 they were within 1.1% at every length (A B B A, eight rounds of prompts from 330 to 27,600 tokens). A first turn keeps more states than on the grid: where the user's message starts, and 512 and 2,048 tokens before the end of a long system prompt. Conversations that share a system prompt share those states; with a different system prompt in every chat, the 16 GiB default kept about the last 100 of 300 two-turn chats ready to resume, where whole-window states left room for about 25, and a second turn resumed 2,607 of its 2,647 tokens instead of 2,048 (medians). Replies equal the cache-off server's token for token, drafted and with `"draft": false`, greedy and seeded sampled, alone and four chats at once (#670). Thanks to @jschmied.
- On CUDA, a kept Nemotron prompt state holds what its prompt needs: each attention layer's and the MTP head's key and value rows up to the prompt's length, and the Mamba states. It used to copy the whole window's caches. For Nemotron 3.5 Lightning a state is 48 MiB of Mamba states plus 7 KiB a prompt token of key and value rows, each part counted in the whole 2 MiB pages the driver allocates: 62 MiB at 2,048 tokens, where every state took 543.7 MiB at a 70,000-token window. The cache keeps four Mamba buffers of its budget free for the next states, 192 MiB of 16 GiB, so a state kept inside a chunk allocates nothing before the chunk runs: a fresh 48 MiB allocation takes 2.1 ms on a GB10. The 16 GiB default held 258 states instead of 30. Replies and decode speed are unchanged.
- Qwen3.8-27B on a Mac keeps its weights in memory once. The checkpoint's projections and LM head stay in their files until load repacks them into the layouts the kernels read, and the 4-bit DFlash2 drafter reads its bf16 layers once, into its 4-bit copies, so a served 27B with its drafter takes 17.6 GiB less from load on: 26.5 against 44.2 GiB on an M5 Max and 21.8 against 39.4 GiB on an M3 Ultra at the default 32,768-token context. Replies are the same tokens, at the same speed. A round the GPU fails, out of memory for example, now ends only the requests it held and logs Metal's error, and later requests run; before, every later request failed (#643). Thanks to @RS91Dev for the report and the analysis.
- GLM-5.3-Flash on one Mac with `GLM_POOL=0`, a window a stream, copies a kept prompt state's prefixes out of its slot before a new stream writes over them, when the prompt cache's budget has room, so that conversation resumes later instead of reading its prompt again. A copy takes about 18 KB of the budget a prompt token. With 8 of the 45 layers loaded and `--parallel 4`, six conversations taking turns resumed 32,531 of 46,465 prompt tokens and no restore failed, where 1.0.7 resumed 15,466 and 6 restores failed; the default shared pool resumes the same 32,531. Every reply was unchanged. The shared pool, whose kept prefixes already hold their blocks, is unchanged (#644). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- GLM-5.3-Flash on two Macs copies kept prompt states out of their slots before a new stream writes over them, as one Mac does with `GLM_POOL=0`, so a busy pair keeps other conversations' prefixes. The first Mac takes the states the prompt cache's budget has room for, never evicting during a prompt pass, and sends their ids; the second copies its share of the same states. Each Mac holds the whole MLA prefix of a copied state, about 18 KB a prompt token. On two M5 Ultras, six conversations taking turns on four streams resumed 32,526 of 46,301 prompt tokens and no restore failed, where 1.0.7 resumed 15,430 and 17,745 in two runs, with 6 and 5 failed restores. After the release gate's exact, chat, concurrency and behaviour phases, a prompt A, an unrelated B and A again resumes 3,165 of A's 3,181 tokens, where 1.0.7 read them all again, and no restore in the session fails, against 4. That was 1.0.6 and 1.0.7's known issue. Replies, drafted, resumed and concurrent alike, equal 1.0.7's, and decode speed is unchanged. It extends #644. Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- A new GLM-5.3-Flash stream takes the least recently used slot: its kept prompt state's own slot when that is free, else a free slot holding no kept state, else the one whose newest kept state was kept or restored longest ago. 1.0.7 took the free slot holding the fewest kept states. With the copies above, the pick decides whose states a new stream copies out, and whose go stale once the prompt cache's budget is full: those used longest ago. It extends #622. Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- `logprobs` on streamed chat completions and on `/v1/completions`, on the native CUDA server. A stream's chunks carry the rows of the tokens their `delta.content` completes, so the rows joined over a stream are the whole reply's, answer-only with thinking on; `/v1/completions` takes the legacy integer `logprobs` (0 to 20) and answers with `tokens`, `token_logprobs`, `top_logprobs` and `text_offset`, streamed or not. On a GB10, the rows joined over each stream equalled the whole reply's bit for bit, and every row stayed within 2e-6 of a float64 reference (#645, #449). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- `prompt_logprobs` on nonstreamed `/v1/completions`, on the native CUDA server, in vLLM's form: `null` for the first prompt token, then each prompt token's `logprob`, `rank` in the whole vocabulary and `decoded_token`, followed by up to 20 best tokens. The prompt pass reads every row through the head on the GPU, so such a request prefills its whole prompt rather than resuming a cached prefix, and chat, streamed requests and other engines refuse the field instead of answering with an empty list. On a GB10, every prompt row stayed within 2.1e-6 of a float64 reference with its rank exact, and `prompt_logprobs: 20` on a 2,931-token prompt took 0.83 s where the same prompt without it took 0.44 s. Requests that ask for neither field keep 1.0.6's tokens and speed (#647, #449). Thanks to @plotarmordev, to @Bizuayeu for the spec in #399 and to @jetnet for asking in #298.
- The opt-in HIP runtime has events, timed or for ordering only, a stream that waits on another stream's event, and the device's free and total memory, as the CUDA runtime does; a query that finds work still queued reports it unfinished instead of failing. Its GPU tests check that a stream waiting on an event reads the value written before it, and they passed on gfx1030, gfx1100, gfx1151 and gfx1201 cards in contributors' runs. No model runs on HIP yet (#630). Thanks to @jkuepker, and to @BlivionIaG and @ThinkOffApp for the runs.
- Chat templates can pass a mapping as keyword arguments, `**mapping`, to macros, functions and filters, as Jinja2 does, with string keys only and as the call's last argument. A non-mapping, a key the call already names, or an argument after the mapping fails as it does in Jinja2, and so does a call that names one keyword twice, which used to take the last value. Nemotron 3.5 Lightning's own template, rewritten with seven such calls and served with `--chat-template`, gave the same token ids as transformers on five conversations and the same replies as its stock template; the server refused it at startup before (#648). Thanks to @akol1.
- An engine can report that it cannot enforce a thinking budget, and the native server then refuses a positive one with a 400 before it opens a stream or hands the request over: the request's `thinking_budget`, `thinking.budget_tokens` on `/v1/messages`, or `--thinking-budget` when the request leaves the field out or sends 0, while thinking is on. No engine in this release reports that, so replies are unchanged; Nemotron's with and without a budget were the same tokens as before. A gate build whose engine reported no support refused whole, streamed and `/v1/messages` requests before the engine saw them, and served budget 0 and thinking off (#649). Thanks to @akol1.
- Whole chat completions keep the prose a model writes before its tool calls in `message.content`, as streamed replies already did; before, `content` was `null` whenever a call was present. Whole `/v1/messages` and `/v1/responses` replies carry it as a text block or a message item before the call, and a stored response keeps it for `previous_response_id`. A reply with only calls still has `null` content, and reply tokens are unchanged: on Nemotron with thinking on, the replies that wrote a sentence before their call were the same tokens before and after, drafted and plain. Qwen3.8-27B's background prefill of the next turn now renders the prose too, so it matches what a streaming client sends back: such a turn resumed 346 of 369 prompt tokens, against 341 before, with the reply a server with the prompt cache off gives (#650). Thanks to @akol1.
- When an engine runs a prompt pass a chunk at a time between rounds, the lane host now gives the next chunk to a foreground prompt before a background one, then to the prompt with the fewest tokens left to run (the oldest of equals), so a short request no longer waits for every chunk of a long prompt admitted ahead of it, and a foreground request no longer waits for a background prompt's whole pass. No engine in this release fills prompts in chunks yet, so on Nemotron the order changes nothing: on a GB10 and an M5 Max every reply equals the build without it, and three short prompts sent 0.2 s after a long one wait for its whole pass with both builds (on the GB10, about 3 s behind 20,000 prompt tokens against 0.11 s alone). In the author's runs on a HIP engine that does fill in chunks (#631), the slowest short request behind 8,192-token prompts got its first token after 0.73 s instead of 11.4 s, and the long prompts finished a little later (#651). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- On CUDA, `sample.cu`'s keyed draws and logprob rows launch through one shared module, `cuda/sample.zig`, so a second CUDA family can draw by the same rule as Nemotron and the Metal engine without importing Nemotron. The kernels and their launches are unchanged: on a GB10 and an RTX PRO 6000, seeded sampled replies over four seeds and five temperature, top_p, top_k and min_p settings, greedy replies, logprob rows and `prompt_logprobs` rows equal the previous build's, and decode speed is unchanged (#654). Thanks to @jschmied.
- The native CUDA server refuses `--learn` at startup, and `--learn-dir` and `--learn-gib`, which turn it on, with one line: `--learn learns prompt prefixes on Macs only: serve without --learn, --learn-dir or --learn-gib on CUDA`. Learned prompt prefixes are kept on disk by the Mac engines only; before, a CUDA server took the flag, kept nothing and said nothing. The refusal comes before the driver opens or the model loads.
- On a Mac, Qwen3.8-27B and Qwen3.5-2B refuse `--learn` at startup too, and `--learn-dir` and `--learn-gib`, which turn it on, with one line that names the model. The 27B's reads `--learn learns prompt prefixes for Nemotron, Flash Next and GLM-5.3-Flash only: serve Qwen3.8-27B without --learn, --learn-dir or --learn-gib`. Neither engine keeps prompt states on disk; before, both took the flags, kept nothing and said nothing. The refusal comes before the model loads.
- A Mac server whose startup fails after its idle keepalive started, out of memory for example, now stops the keepalive and frees what it took before the engine closes. Before, the keepalive's thread went on committing command buffers to the engine's queue while the engine was freed under it.
- On a Mac, the prompt cache's default budget stops at 16 GiB for Nemotron, Flash Next and Qwen3.8-27B, so a server leaves the rest of the memory to other apps. It used to take all that 70% of RAM leaves once the server has loaded, less 2 GiB for prompt buffers: on a 128 GB M5 Max, 68.9 GiB for Nemotron and 45.9 GiB for the 27B with its drafter. Where less is free, the default is still all of it. `--prompt-cache-gib` still sets any size up to that room, or past it with `--prompt-cache-over-cap`, and `0` turns the cache off. The startup line says so, for example `prompt cache: 16.0 GiB from 68.9 GiB free under the 70% cap (...); 16 GiB by default: --prompt-cache-gib takes more, up to what is free`. Replies are unchanged: on the M5 Max, Nemotron's drafted replies equalled `"draft": false` and the previous build's tokens on 17 prompts, greedy, sampled and thinking, and two chats resumed from the cache equalled the cache-off server's. GLM-5.3-Flash and Nemotron on CUDA already kept 16 GiB by default.
- On a Mac, GLM-5.3-Flash's shared cache pool no longer holds every request behind one that waits for room. A request that fits only once the running streams leave used to stop the whole queue; now a later request that fits beside the running streams goes first, and once the streams the waiting request waits on have ended nothing passes it, so it still runs next. With 8 of the 45 layers on an M5 Ultra at `--context 8192`, three short requests sent behind a 6,000-token request that fits only alone answered in 0.2 to 0.3 s while a 3,000-token reply ran, where they waited 12.3 to 12.5 s for that reply to end. In the same queue, a request the engine has no memory for yet is tried again whenever another request ends, where the engine used to keep the lane count it had at that moment for good; one with nothing else running fails at once with a message instead of keeping the engine busy forever; and a structured request that waits for memory frees its grammar each time. Replies are the same tokens.
- Qwen3.8-27B and GLM-5.3-Flash answer every turn whose prompt and `max_tokens` fit `--context`. The server gives a request that leaves `max_tokens` out the rest of the window, so each turn of a conversation within 4,096 tokens of `--context` asks for all of it. The 27B refused anything past `--context` less 16 rows, and GLM's shared pool, which keeps whole 256-token blocks, anything past 8,171 tokens at `--context 8192`. Both refusals came after the server had accepted the turn, as HTTP 500 (`the prompt and max_tokens do not fit this server's --context`), which clients retry instead of compacting. The 27B's slots now hold 16 rows past `--context` for a last draft window, and GLM reports the window its pool holds, so the server clamps `max_tokens` to it and refuses past it with a context-length error. At `--context 8192`, a 7,237-token prompt without `max_tokens` got its 60-token reply from the 27B on an M5 Max, and a 7,264-token one its 907 tokens from GLM on an M5 Ultra, where both got a 500. The 27B's replies are the same tokens (34 exact and 12 chat replies on an M3 Ultra), and its slots take 1 MiB more each.
- A Qwen3.8-27B stream that needs a slot no stream has used yet frees kept prompt states first, least valuable first and never the one it resumes, before it waits for another stream to end. Kept states are Metal buffers of their own, so a full prompt cache could hold the memory a new slot needs and keep later requests waiting for the slots already in use. With the 16 GiB default that takes a Mac with less memory or a larger `--prompt-cache-gib`: Metal leaves 107.5 GiB of an M5 Max's 128 GiB, so it does not happen there. A slot that still does not fit logs what it needs and what is free.
- GLM-5.3-Flash on one Mac resumes a long conversation's next turn from its kept prefix. When the shared pool had no free run long enough for the new turn beside the kept prefixes, it gave prefixes up oldest first, the one being resumed included, so a conversation longer than half of `--context` read its whole prompt again whenever another conversation's prefix was kept. Now the turn starts on its own prefix's blocks when nothing else lies there. With 8 of the 45 layers at `--context 262144`, conversations of 110k, 150k and 190k tokens one after another resumed 109,565, 149,983 and 190,067 tokens on their second turns in 0.3 to 0.4 s, where the build before resumed only the first and read the other two again (14 and 18 s). The replies were the same tokens.
- The server binary takes Zig's std options from the backend built into it when the backend declares them, and the defaults otherwise. The HIP backend needs this for RCCL at `--tp 2`: RCCL and HIP start threads of their own, which Zig's per-thread alternate signal stack leaves too little room to start, so that backend can turn the stack off for its own binary (with the RCCL work in #655). Metal and CUDA declare none and build as before (#696). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The cluster layer's leader refuses to draw from a row of logits holding a NaN or an infinity, greedy or sampled, with `NonfiniteLogits`, as Qwen's draws already do. A NaN broke the sampler's sort order, which panicked in safe builds and could crash a fast one, and other such rows still drew a token, sometimes the NaN's own id. Only the cluster's tests build a leader so far, so no served model reaches it yet (#658). Thanks to @chaog992.
- A streamed `/v1/messages` reply's `message_start` carries the prompt's size in `usage.input_tokens`: the whole prompt the engine runs, after `--compact-at` compaction when that applies, with the cache fields 0. It used to be 0, so a client that reads the size there showed an empty context. The final `message_delta` keeps the engine's split into `input_tokens` and `cache_read_input_tokens`, as before. On Nemotron, on an M5 Max and a GB10, the head matched `count_tokens` and the delta's sum on every streamed request, and with `--compact-at` it named the compacted prompt the engine ran (#672). Thanks to @kky42.
- Nemotron, on Macs and on CUDA, keeps prompt states for prompts under 4,096 tokens, so a short conversation's next turn resumes from where the last turn's history ended instead of reading the whole prompt again. The 4,096-token floor is there because keeping a state can cost an extra prompt call, but Nemotron keeps states without one: on a Mac at chunk starts its prompt pass makes anyway, as GLM-5.3-Flash already does, and on CUDA at any row (see the first entry above). On an M5 Max, turns 2 to 5 of chats under 2,800 tokens started in 0.19 to 0.31 s instead of 0.66 to 0.85 s; CUDA's figures are in the two entries above. Replies are the same tokens, and a first turn's prompt pass on a Mac takes about 0.4% longer to keep its state. Short chats now fill the prompt cache up to its budget (16 GiB by default) and evict as long ones do: a state is about 60 MiB on a Mac and 50 to 62 MiB on CUDA. Flash Next and Qwen3.8-27B keep the floor (#669). Thanks to @jschmied.
- Tool calls inside a legacy DSML `tool_calls` envelope stay inside it, in whole replies and streams alike. A `<tool_call>` or JSON call written in an envelope's arguments is an argument, and one in an envelope the reply never closes stays text; before, it became a call of its own, and a stream sent it in place of the envelope's own call. A stream holds a DSML envelope until it closes and then sends its calls whole, and the calls around it keep their order. A whole reply that is one bare JSON call keeps any tag text in its arguments, and a streamed reply that starts with `{`, `[` or a code fence now gets its calls whole at the end instead of as argument deltas. XML calls such as Nemotron's stream as before (#684). Thanks to @akol1.
- Flash Next keeps thinking budgets. A request's `thinking_budget`, `thinking.budget_tokens` on `/v1/messages`, or `--thinking-budget` now ends Flash Next's thinking at the budget, as on the other engines. The budget's last token becomes the start of the chat template's close, `\n</think>\n\n`, and the answer follows. A reply that ends its thinking before the budget is unchanged. Before, Flash Next accepted a budget and ignored it, so a client that always sends one got the model's full thinking. Drafting stays on. The close lands inside a drafted window, and replies under a budget equal `"draft": false` token for token, whole and streamed. In speed-up mode the first Mac passes the budget to the second with each request. On an M5 Ultra with the 6-bit checkpoint and thinking on, a 64-token budget ended the thinking of the three of four prompts that think longer at 64 tokens (65 reasoning tokens, counting `</think>`) and a 256-token budget the one that thinks past it, each followed by its answer, and `/v1/messages` gave the same thinking and text. Replies without a budget, or with 0, are the same tokens as before, at the same speed. #649 brought the gap to light, and #701 reported it with a repro (136 reasoning tokens under a 32-token budget; 33 now); thanks to @akol1 and @dinadur.
- The core lane projection prepares the input sums of every quantization group. Its sums pass launched threads for K / 64 groups whatever the group size, so a group-32 projection with prepared sums read unwritten sums for half its groups and got their bias terms wrong, and `zig build test-core-lane` failed its cooperative 4-bit group-32 check on an M5 Max (#675). The check now passes all 115 layouts on an M5 Max and an M5 Ultra, and of those only the 16 cooperative group-32 layouts with prepared sums give different bytes from before. No shipped model takes that path. Qwen3.8-27B and its DFlash2 drafter prepare sums on 4-bit group-64 weights, which launch the same threads as before, and Flash Next's 6-bit group-32 projections add their sums inline, so replies are unchanged. On an M5 Max the 27B's replies to 17 release prompts, greedy, sampled and with thinking on, were the same tokens before and after (#698). Thanks to @cwschroeder.
- Flash Next with `FZ_LANE=1`, which the speed-up guide sets, loads on Macs without tensor units (M1 to M4). Its target layers' core lane projections stopped the server at load there with `TensorUnitsRequired`. Their 6-bit group-32 layout now runs on simdgroup matrices, through the fragment layout Flash Next's prompt kernels already use on those Macs, and the core projection's other layouts still need tensor units. On an M3 Ultra the projection's outputs on Flash Next's 13 layouts are byte-equal to an M5 Max's, and the author's served runs on an M3 Ultra matched the default route's tokens. Macs with tensor units give the same bytes as before on all 115 checked layouts, and on an M5 Ultra Flash Next's replies with `FZ_LANE=1 FZ_GDN=2` were the same tokens as before (#679). Thanks to @cwschroeder.
- A Qwen3.8-27B server with the DFlash2 drafter starts with a small `--context`. A new build's first start times each draft window width in a scratch context the size of the server's: a 192-token prompt, then at least 133 timed windows that each keep a row, and twice as many when the cost cache holds an older build's timings more than 15% away. In a small context the scratch context ran out, and the server refused to start with `BadRoundPlan` until a start at a larger context had saved the timings. The scratch prompt now leaves room for the widest window, and the scratch context is filled again before a window that would not fit. On an M3 Ultra, `--context 256` now starts in 11.2 s from an empty cost cache, and `--context 448` and 256 start in 18 to 19 s from a cache holding an older build's timings; the build before refused all three. Drafted replies there equal `"draft": false`. At the default context the timings are the same as before, each width within 0.4 ms (0.8%), while two starts of one build differ by up to 2.7% (#661). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- The Qwen3.8-27B checks that compare served replies with the command line's tokens and state (`tf-qwen27-served-check`, `tf-qwen27-dflash-served-check`, `tf-qwen27-lanes-check`, `tf-qwen27-lanes-run` and `tf-qwen27-reuse`) drive the host the server opens, so they test what ships. They used to build against a single-stream host that no server has opened since 1.0.6; it is gone, with the drafter's batch delivery that only it used. Their contracts follow the served host: sampled and custom-stop requests draft, a cancel lands at the end of a round, a prompt-only request is a warm pass, and on the lane core, whose draft trees follow every reply the server has drafted, a resumed reply must equal a fresh one in tokens and final state but not in rounds. The drafted check starts at a 256-token context, so a new build's first run goes through the timing refill above. All five pass on an M3 Ultra and an M5 Max, on the lane core and on the M5's serial loop, where the old drafted check failed its cancel cases on the lane core. Served replies are unchanged (#659). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- On CUDA, Nemotron's structured-output windows replay a captured graph, as other windows do, instead of launching each kernel from the host. That covers JSON schemas, `json_object`, `guided_regex`, `guided_choice` and tool calls under `tool_choice`. A sequence captures a masked window's graph for each row count and draw mode the first time it runs one, and the grammar's bits sit at one device address that the host rewrites before each launch. On a GB10, 26 structured cells gave the same tokens as before in four runs; the 9,426 windows a run launched eagerly now replay graphs after 22 captures (about 50 ms in all), and decode under a grammar is 4.4% faster with `"draft": false` (3.0 to 5.4% by cell) and 1.0% faster drafted. Decode without a grammar is unchanged (x1.000 to x1.002), and with `--parallel 2` a second stream's masked windows replay graphs of its own (#660). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Flash Next in speed-up mode gives a `"draft": false` request the same reply every time, and the two Macs no longer give different replies. Each Mac's host thread sends what its GPU computed to the other Mac, and when that thread ran late, which a busy Mac makes common, two races could change a reply. In rounds of one row, as every round of a `"draft": false` request is, the first Mac wrote each DeltaNet layer's partial sum into the buffer the previous layer's partial could still be leaving from, so the second Mac could add the wrong partial; on one cable the link could also fail with `a malformed header message`. Consecutive layers' partials now alternate between two buffers, and a buffer is written again only after the other Mac's next partial, which it sends once it has read this one, has arrived. And the host thread sized each message by the row count in the GPU's latest message rather than its own, so a drafted round's last picks could go out as a single row and the second Mac merged stale picks for the others; each message now carries its own size. On two M5 Ultras with 36 busy threads on the first Mac, `"draft": false` replies differed from the drafted ones on 43 of 48 requests before, the two Macs' replies differed on 18 of 96, and on one cable the link failed in both tries. After, 70 such requests all gave the drafted reply, the same on both Macs wherever both were checked, with the prompt cache on and off, `FZ_LANE=1 FZ_GDN=2`, `MCDMA_FABRIC_QOS=1`, one cable or four, fresh servers and resumed conversations. Without load the replies, drafted and not, are the same tokens as before, and decode speed is unchanged: x1.000 prose and x1.001 code on the default route, x0.997 and x1.000 with the guide's `FZ_LANE=1 FZ_GDN=2 MCDMA_FABRIC_QOS=1`.
- Living Weights takes out a lesson that makes the model answer a question about the user it was never told: six such questions (a cat's or dog's name, "Do you remember my name?") are asked after every round and the mining rounds and never trained, and five "Do you remember...?" questions about other untold things are trained to keep their answers. On an M5 Max, "I like blue." then stays learned without turning "Do you remember my name?" into "I remember your name is Alex.", and lessons that answered "What's the name of my cat?" with "Whiskers" are taken out (#657). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- Living Weights learns facts about the user more reliably. Ten told in turn ("I like blue.", "My sister is called Ana.", "My dog is called Ginger.", "I live in Bristol.", "My birthday is on 14 March.", "I work as a nurse.", "My favourite food is sushi.", "I drive a Volvo.", "My son is called Leo." and "I am allergic to peanuts.") bring back 18 of their 20 questions on an M5 Ultra, against 12 in 1.0.7, and 13 to 16 on a GB10 across two runs, against 10; a plain restart keeps them. 1.0.7 refused "I live in Bristol." because the model named the whole sentence as its subject, so every question had to say Bristol; such a fact now keys on the words its questions use. The questions about the user that every lesson keeps steady are read again once a fact answers them (a later lesson was taken out for knowing an earlier birthday), a lesson never also checks one of its own questions, and a held-out question never brings in a word the fact does not say ("What name is my little sister?").
- A fact sent as chat about someone or something other than the user is learned as a document's fact is, and one naming a person beside an organisation is learned both ways round, so "Who is the head of quality at Brightwell Foods?" and "What does Ilse Marwood do?" both reach it. A tester's six facts about two made-up companies, sent as chat, bring back 10 of their 14 questions on an M5 Ultra and 9 on a GB10, against 1 (one the model knew already) and 3 in 1.0.7, which refused most of them for too few clean answers.
- A fact told in another language is learned with that language's own words for I and my and its own questions, and an answer in another case counts ("w Gdańsku" for Gdańsk). The tester's two Polish facts come back 3 of 4 times, asked in Polish and in English, on both machines, against none in 1.0.7.
- A newer chat fact that changes or takes back an earlier one replaces it: the earlier fact's neurons are zeroed before the lesson and put back if the new fact is not learned, and its answers leave what later lessons keep steady. After "I like blue.", "My favourite colour is red." and then "I do not like blue anymore. Now I like red.", "What's my favourite colour?" says red on both machines, where 1.0.7 kept saying blue. A newer fact that only says an earlier one again does not replace it (#678).
- Documents: a 20-fact company handbook taught in four parts brings back 13 to 15 of its questions on an M5 Ultra (three handbooks), against 8 in 1.0.7, and 13 to 15 on a GB10 across three runs, against 10 in 1.0.7 on the same handbook. Each fact is also asked for the other way round and kept only when the model says both say the same, a subject's earlier answers are replayed in its later lessons, and lessons are written from the model as shipped.
- A fact's subject phrase is cut at its verb, so "Our company's warehouse robot is called Pebble." is learned (every question had to say Pebble before), and a question naming the subject in the singular or plural counts, so "What is my eye colour?" is learned for "My eyes are green." (#687, #678).
- Nemotron's prompt pass on Macs reads each token's row and writes the experts' output rows in place, so it no longer copies rows three times a layer: prompts are 0.9-1.2% faster on an M3 Ultra and 2-5% on an M5 Max from 2k to 32k tokens, with the same bits.
- A Metal EXL3 projection for model code to call, and `tf-exl3-bench`, which checks it against FP64 dots on synthetic trellis weights at every codebook and width and times it. It reads ExLlamaV3's stored tiles from the caller's buffers, for any number of rows and for routed experts, with BF16 or F32 out. No model uses it yet. On an M5 Max and an M3 Ultra its outputs equal its scalar decoder's bit for bit in all 55 qualification cases (#681, proposal #677). Thanks to @akol1.
- `tensorfold pull REPO@REVISION --exlr8 PROFILE --node R/N --dir DIR` fetches one Mac's share of an EXLR8 release. EXLR8 is kindlingai's trellis-coded format: every routed expert is stored at three widths (K2, K3 and K4), and each profile's manifest picks one for every expert half. Node R of N fetches every whole file and only its own halves of each expert, by range. Each whole file must equal the hub's sha256, each expert file's published hash the hub's own, and each expert half its xxhash64 before it is written, and a pull that stops resumes with what is missing. For `kindlingai/glm-5.3-exlr8-k2-k3-k4`'s `home` profile on two nodes that is 139.5 GiB of expert halves and 11 GB of whole files a Mac. The reader decodes what the format's reference loader decodes, bit for bit: the expert tiles to the same E4M3 values and fp32 weights on layers 0, 3, 40 and 78 and the vocabulary, and the int4, MXFP8, FP8 and bf16 dense matrices to the same fp32. Its Metal and CUDA kernels sum in one order, so their outputs are byte-equal on an M5 Max, an M3 Ultra, an RTX PRO 6000 and a GB10. Thanks to @akol1, whose Metal EXL3 projection (#681, above) landed with this work.
- GLM-5.3 (`glm_moe_dsa`) serves on two Macs from that EXLR8 release with `serve --speed-up`: rank 0 serves and rank 1 runs each request beside it. Each Mac holds half of every expert and attention head, 157.2 GB of weights with the MTP layer and 159.6 GB at `--context 8192 --parallel 4`, and the server refuses a load past 66% of RAM. On two M5 Ultras over one cable, one stream decodes at 21 tokens a second with `"draft": false` and 31 to 32 with the MTP layer's drafts, and reads prompts at about 200 tokens a second (205 at 837 tokens, 184 at 3,251). Drafted replies equal `"draft": false`, a resumed conversation equals a fresh one, and concurrent requests equal their solo replies, idle and with 36 busy threads on each Mac. It decodes greedily and refuses `--learn` and `--slide`. No CUDA engine serves it yet, and replies past 2,048 tokens, where the sparse indexer starts choosing which keys each query reads, have not been compared with a whole-model reference. [GLM-5.3 on two Macs](docs/glm-5.3-exlr8.md) has the pull and serve commands.
- Two-Mac servers keep their decode speed while other work keeps a Mac busy. The MCDMA library lands each arriving message on a CPU thread at default priority unless `MCDMA_FABRIC_QOS=1` is set, and the threads that send Flash Next's and GLM-5.3-Flash's exchanges ran at default priority too, so busy CPUs delayed every exchange while both GPUs waited. Every two-Mac server now sets `MCDMA_FABRIC_QOS=1` before it opens the link, unless the variable is already set, and Flash Next and GLM-5.3-Flash send from user-interactive threads, as GLM-5.3's pair does. On two M5 Ultras over one cable with 36 busy threads on the first Mac, Flash Next's `"draft": false` replies decoded at 104.5 tokens a second (median of 16), level with an idle pair's 104.7, where the MCDMA setting alone gave 97.5 and `MCDMA_FABRIC_QOS=0` 64.5, and drafted replies were level with idle cell by cell. GLM-5.3-Flash's `"draft": false` replies decoded at 80.1 tokens a second under the same load, against 87.9 idle and 37.4 with the MCDMA setting alone, and its drafted replies at 122.6 against 123.6. Replies are the same tokens as before, drafted, `"draft": false` and resumed, and the two Macs agree on every one. The speed-up guide no longer has a Python dump step, since the server loads Flash Next from its checkpoint, or `FZ_LANE=1 FZ_GDN=2`, which decode 2 to 5% slower than the default kernels, and its speed tables are measured again on the default route.
- On a Mac, every model but Nemotron refuses `--slide` at startup, as a CUDA server refuses it for a model without a learner: `--slide: qwen4_exp checkpoints do not learn on Macs yet`. Living Weights learns into Nemotron 3.5 Lightning only; Flash Next, GLM-5.3-Flash, Qwen3.8-27B and Qwen3.5-2B took the flag and learned nothing. GLM-5.3 (EXLR8) refuses `--slide` the same way, and `--learn`, `--learn-dir` and `--learn-gib` as the 27B and the 2B do. Both refusals come before the model loads.

## 1.0.7

- Living Weights learns each fact into about ten new neurons inside the model's own shards, on Macs and on a GB10. Every MoE layer's shared expert gets a pocket of 64 extra neurons; a fact takes one at each of about ten layers, fired at the first row of the reply (a fact about you) or at the subject's last token where a question names it (a fact about someone or something), with outputs fitted through the model so the answers come back (with thinking on, 4 of 6 test wordings on a GB10 and 5 of 6 on an M5 Ultra in the release check), while near misses, free writing about the subject and other answers stay as they were. Nothing the model had is changed, no adapter is loaded, and any MLX loader reads the folder as a slightly wider model. A fact is kept only when held-out wordings bring it back and nothing else moved; otherwise it is taken out and leaves nothing behind. Documents are learned fact by fact: the model finds the facts, names the organisation on each, and keeps only those whose names, numbers and words the text holds (a list holding others is read again), then writes questions that name their subject (rewriting ones that ask for it), and leaves a question another fact answers to that fact. Three personal facts taught one after another each came back on both test questions on an M5 Ultra, an M3 Ultra and a GB10, with no leaks and no drift, live and after a plain restart; a six-fact handbook gave 3 to 6 of 6 answers across six runs, and long documents about one subject are still weak (6 of 16 from a 21-fact handbook). Without learned facts, replies and speed equal 1.0.6's on both backends; a folder with 21 facts decodes about 16% slower on a Mac. `--slide` refuses a folder taught by 1.0.3 to 1.0.6, whose lessons sit in the shared experts the pocket widens; such folders still serve without it. It builds on the CUDA learner and the take-out rule of #591, #599 and #632. Thanks to @ZackSample and @ThinkOffApp.
- Living Weights learns about four times faster. A lesson's training passes hold up to 32 answers side by side, each answer's prompt runs once and later passes start after it, the neurons' input fit reads its rows in order, the routed experts' and the scan's backward run as tiles on both backends, and the CUDA trainer reads its weights directly (ready in about 3 s instead of 26). The learning rounds for "I like blue." take 52 s on an M3 Ultra (217 s before) and 47 s on a GB10 (200 s before); a whole fact, lesson writing included, takes about 1.5 to 2.5 minutes on an M5 Ultra or M3 Ultra and 2.5 to 3 minutes on a GB10, with the same recall. Packed passes equal one-at-a-time passes to the bit on CUDA; on Metal a packed pass with more rows takes the engine's prompt kernels, as long prompts do.
- `/slide` draws what the model has learned in 3D: the facts as a graph around the model's 52 layers, each fact's neurons lit in its pocket, the shard files that hold them, and each lesson played live as it is written, read, fitted and checked. `GET /v1/slide/graph` adds each fact's neurons, round, loss and phase, and the model's layers, pockets and shards. The page loads three.js 0.169.0 from jsDelivr, pinned by integrity hash, and falls back to the flat view without it.

## 1.0.6

- Nemotron on CUDA: a request on a sequence other than the engine's own captures its own CUDA graphs the first time it runs each step and replays them after, instead of running every window eagerly. On a GB10, three concurrent 7k-token conversations, which crashed the earlier relocation design, ran clean in every run, drafted and concurrent replies equalled plain and solo ones, and one-stream decode and prompts were unchanged (#615, #597). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Structured output on the native server. Nemotron enforces `response_format` (json_object, json_schema) and vLLM's `guided_json`, `guided_regex`, `guided_choice`, `guided_grammar` and `structured_outputs` on Metal and CUDA, and Qwen3.5-2B on Metal. Drafting stays on: the head's drafts come back from the GPU before each verify, the grammar cuts the ones it rejects and drafts the text it forces itself (keys, closing markup), and each row's logits are masked before its draw, so every constrained reply equals its `"draft": false` reply token for token, alone or beside other streams, and replies without a grammar are unchanged. With thinking off, Nemotron decodes JSON-schema replies at 245 to 290 tok/s on an M3 Ultra and 645 to 765 on an RTX PRO 6000, and required tool calls at 290 to 320 and 790 to 955, two and a half to six tokens a round; the grammar path itself costs 2 to 7%. xgrammar v0.2.8 loads at runtime from `lib/libtfgrammar`, which each release archive ships; without it, a structured request gets a 400 and everything else serves (#596, #564). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- `tool_choice: "required"` and named functions work on the native server, on the same engines: the answer is one or more calls in the chat template's own markup, each to an offered tool with its arguments inside the tool's schema, and `parallel_tool_calls: false` allows exactly one. Before, the native server refused them with a 400.
- `--learn` keeps Flash Next's shared prompt prefixes, such as a system prompt and its tools, on disk on one Mac, so a new server resumes them without reading them again. On an M5 Ultra with the 6-bit checkpoint, a fresh server found two learned states and resumed 5,591 of a 6,122-token first prompt from disk, and every reply equalled the cache-off server's. A speed-up pair refuses `--learn` for now (#519). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- GLM-5.3-Flash on one Mac shares one MLA cache across its streams: a request takes a region of the window for its prompt and `max_tokens` and gives it back when it leaves, and one that does not fit waits in the queue until enough streams leave. A lone request can still use the whole window. Kept prompt prefixes hold their blocks until a stream needs the room. With 8 of the 45 layers loaded, a 1M-token window and three streams took 48 GB against 85 GB before, with every reply unchanged. `GLM_POOL=0` keeps a window each, and the two-Mac pair is unchanged (#639). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- A GLM load drops only as much of the checkpoint's file cache as free memory lacks, and skips shards with nothing cached, so the rest stays cached and a load with nothing cached makes no pass. On two M5 Ultras with the whole checkpoint cached, each Mac dropped 36 to 100 GiB where 1.0.5 dropped all 169 GiB, and a load served in 10 to 18 s against 14 to 20 s. Every reply was unchanged (#640). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- The Linux archives carry every CUDA kernel for sm_120 as well as sm_86 and sm_121, so RTX 50-series and RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell cards run the native engine. Before, they stopped at kernel load with `CUDA_ERROR_NO_BINARY_FOR_GPU`. `zig build dist` now reads each fatbin and refuses one without an sm_86, sm_120 or sm_121 image. On an RTX PRO 6000, Nemotron 3.5 Lightning MLX 4-bit loads in 15 s and decodes greedy at 875 tok/s on code and 607 on prose with drafts, and drafted replies equal plain ones, greedy and sampled (#610). Thanks to @Nipale-ai for the report and the RTX 5090 receipt.
- The JSON writer turns a UTF-8 sequence cut off at a string's end into U+FFFD, where it used to read past the string (#634). Thanks to @jayleaton.
- Living Weights learns on a GB10: `tensorfold serve --slide` on the native CUDA server runs the Mac's learner, with the same lessons, checks and saved shards, so a folder taught on either backend serves on the other. Every CUDA training kernel matched a float64 reference within 2.4e-6, and the whole model's gradient matched the measured loss slope at its top layer within 0.15%. Without `--slide`, replies are the same tokens and decode is unchanged; with it, a decoded token on a GB10 costs about 1% more with no lessons, 6% more with one kept lesson and 11% more with four. On a GB10 a training step takes 48 to 436 ms and a fact two to four minutes. `--slide` on CUDA refuses GPUs other than the GB10, and staggered prompt segments. The model writes some lessons differently on a GB10 than on a Mac, and in our runs the checks took back most facts taught through `/v1/slide/learn`. (#591, closes #590). Thanks to @ZackSample.
- A lesson taught with `--slide` on CUDA repeats run to run: the training step's split products add their slices in a fixed order instead of by `atomicAdd`, so the same fact on the same folder takes the same steps, passes the same checks and saves the same bytes, at the same step time (#632). Thanks to @ZackSample.
- A NaN or infinite value the server writes the way Python's `str()` does, such as a tool parameter or a decision value, no longer crashes the server (#604). Thanks to @chaog992.
- Nested token ids in a completion prompt that fall outside the vocabulary, or are booleans, get a 400 as top-level ids do. Before, the server dropped them or read them as other ids (#619). Thanks to @chaog992.
- The opt-in AMD HIP build accepts RDNA2 (gfx1030) and RDNA3 (gfx1100) cards, and a capabilities table from RDNA2 to RDNA4, keyed by exact gfx name, tells kernels what each GPU can do. No model runs on HIP yet (#607, #609). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- GLM-5.3-Flash on two Macs times its draft windows when it loads (kept per build; about 6 s on a build's first load) instead of pricing them from a fixed table, so prose settles at one draft a round and code at two. On two M5 Ultras six prose prompts decode at a median of 114.8 tok/s against 110.6 in 1.0.5 and 112.8 in 1.0.4. A 256-token story decodes at 116.5 against 106.1 and 111.5. Code, plain decode and prompts are unchanged. This was 1.0.5's known issue.
- GLM loads drop the checkpoint's pages from the file cache before reading it (no root needed), so a load right after a download or copy no longer waits on free memory. With the whole 169 GiB checkpoint cached on two M5 Ultras, a load served in 16 s, where 1.0.5 had not served after 5 minutes. The pass adds about 2.5 s to a load (#600). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- A new GLM stream takes its kept state's own slot when that slot is free, else the free slot holding the fewest kept states, so an unrelated request no longer overwrites another conversation's kept prefix. After a prompt A, an unrelated B and A again, the third request resumes 3,165 of its 3,181 tokens, where 1.0.5 read them all again (#622). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Living Weights keeps a lesson only when every fact came back. One that misses its held-out questions, or disturbs another answer in its rounds or after its mining rounds, is taken out whole before anything is written, and `learned` says `"recalled": false` with the reason. Before, the learner's undo went back one round, and a lesson that ended `recalled: false` was still written into the shards (#599, fixes #589). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- NVIDIA's NVFP4 checkpoint of Nemotron 3.5 Lightning serves on the native CUDA engine. Its FP8 Mamba projections, NVFP4 experts and head, and bf16 attention load as stored, while activations and the KV cache stay bf16, about 10x closer to a float64 reference than NVIDIA's own math on the FP8 layers. A new staged NVFP4 expert kernel reads prompts as fast as the MLX 4-bit checkpoint on a GB10 (6,090 to 7,270 tok/s from 2k to 32k tokens). Decode runs at 105 to 174 tok/s drafted and 77 to 79 serial, against 133 to 189 and 98 to 101 for MLX 4-bit, because the FP8 and bf16 layers make a token read 1.3x the bytes. Drafted, resumed and concurrent replies equal plain ones token for token. Needs sm_90 or newer. It builds on #595, #629 and #633 from @jschmied.
- For contributors: weight formats behind one interface, MLX affine 2- to 8-bit and dense, with tensor-parallel cuts (#612); a kernel registry that picks a product's tile by tuned cost (#613); a prompt pass of one chunk a round, between the other streams' rounds (#623); serve flags a backend opts into (#624); a paged prompt cache, a radix tree over the backend's own KV pages (#625); what a model engine needs from the HIP runtime (#611); the CUDA affine-4 repack beside its format (#602) and grouped experts behind one call (#605); `zig fmt` on three files (#616). Thanks to @BlivionIaG, @jschmied and @BobClawblaw.

## 1.0.5

- The CUDA `tensorfold` command built from source runs `models`, `info` and `pull` without opening the driver, and `--version` prints the build's version. `pull` refuses a hub file path that would leave the snapshot, and only a 40-character git sha names a snapshot directory (#576). Thanks to @eleqtrizit.
- A request that sets no `top_k`, on a checkpoint whose generation config sets none, keeps every token on CUDA as on Metal, so one seeded request samples the same on a Mac and on a GB10. `--top-k 20` gives 0.6.6's CUDA default. #542 had made 20 the CUDA default; it was taken back for one default everywhere (#515). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- `/metrics` reports pinned host memory on CUDA as `tensorfold:pinned_memory_bytes` (#541, fixes #538), and counts prompt tokens restored from kept prompt states (Flash Next, GLM-5.3-Flash and the Qwen3.8-27B) as `prompt_tokens_cached_total`, repeated as vLLM's `prefix_cache_hits_total` and `prefix_cache_queries_total` (#531, fixes #530). Thanks to @GustavBlack and @juliankang4.
- A request keeps its own copy of its API key's label when the key file is reloaded under it (#560), and the server frees its key store and request counters when it stops (#513). Thanks to @chaog992 and @CerebralCoding.
- `zig build test` on Linux runs the HTTP server's unit tests, and `zig build test-server-cpu` runs them alone (#543). Thanks to @MiaAI-Lab.
- `logprob.cu` compiles with nvcc 13.3 again (#539, fixes #535). Thanks to @GustavBlack.
- The native Qwen3.5-2B serves checkpoints that ship their own `lm_head` as well as tied ones, and refuses a config whose tiedness disagrees with its tensors (#493). A `qwen3_5` checkpoint now opens the 2B engine by its geometry, so an untied 2B no longer reaches the 27B's loader. Thanks to @akol1.
- The `--dashboard` page shows a decode/prefill dial, memory, tokens-in-flight and MTP acceptance tiles, and a five-minute decode-rate chart (#561). Thanks to @heitke.
- The lane core takes an external drafter, such as an EAGLE-3 head or a DFlash block, in front of any target backend, and a stream with drafts off never reaches it. No served family uses it yet (#455). Thanks to @jschmied.
- An opt-in HIP runtime for AMD GPUs with one exact MLX affine 4-bit row projection, and no model on it yet: its mock-backed admission tests run in `zig build test`, and `zig build hip-gpu-test` and `hip-affine-test` run on gfx1150, gfx1151 or gfx1201 (`-Dhip-arch`) (#463). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp, with @BlivionIaG, @jkuepker and @millaguie.
- RUNBOOK.md names no stale release, describes today's CUDA qualification (GB10, and Ampere 8.6 cards) and built-in kernels, and drops the 1.0.0 gaps: a sampled CUDA request honours its seed, `top_k: 0` turns the filter off, and `/metrics` reports device and pinned memory (#537). Thanks to @GustavBlack.
- The speed-up guide and recipe say their two-Mac numbers come from a build with a recorded dump, and that the release binary's dump-free two-Mac path has not passed that gate yet (#567). The recipe book says its capability table is the Python 0.6.6 engine's and names the native CUDA floor, and the RUNBOOK gives the `top_k` default.
- A flag or checkpoint the native engine does not serve is refused with the Python 0.6.6 install line, instead of an `--engine python` flag the native binary does not have.
- The native server accepts `--chat-template FILE` to override the checkpoint template for ordinary and tool-bearing chat. Invalid files fail startup without a fallback (#551). Thanks to @akol1.
- CUDA and GLM recipes distinguish Python 0.6.6 features from native Zig support and link to each engine's runbook (#534). Thanks to @akol1.
- The tokenizer gives a token's exact bytes when its decoder chain is token-local, a building block for constrained decoding (#532). Thanks to @akol1.
- Safetensors headers keep rank-five tensor shapes, such as a vision patch embedding's temporal convolution, and refuse rank six (#533). Thanks to @akol1.
- A backend-neutral EXL3 trellis reference decoder with an oracle fixture, which decodes rectangular layers at the right row stride; no model loads EXL3 on the native engine yet (#496). Thanks to @akol1.
- Flash Next on two Macs bounds each wait for the other rank: a handoff that never arrives fails the request after 10 seconds instead of spinning forever, and a failure or stop on either side ends the wait (#512). Thanks to @321sssrt-bit.
- Nemotron on Metal keeps each conversation's prompt state between requests, so a later turn prefills only its new tokens, and `/metrics` counts the restored tokens. Every reply equals the cache-off reply. On an M3 Ultra the second turn of a 19.8k-token chat prefilled in 0.16 s instead of 8.1 s. `--prompt-cache-gib` sizes the memory as for Flash Next, and `0` turns it off (#497). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- `--learn` keeps Nemotron's shared prompt prefixes, such as a system prompt and its tools, on disk, so a new server resumes them without reading them again. Weights that `--slide` writes into the model's files start a new set (#499). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Nemotron's shared rounds hold four rows a lane, up to 128, so 32 and 64 sessions keep drafting: on an M3 Ultra, 64 sessions went from 376 to 396 tok/s in all. `--parallel 32` and `64` use 3 GiB more memory, `--parallel 8` the same (#518). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Nemotron's routed experts take three member rows a pass in windows and shared rounds, with the same bits: 10-15% faster at 64 and 128 rows on an M3 Ultra, and 64 sessions reach 409 tok/s with #518 (#502). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Nemotron on CUDA keeps prompt states between turns: a conversation's next turn resumes from the state kept at the last 2,048-token chunk boundary before its history, and its reply equals the same request with the cache off (#467). On a GB10, a resumed turn of a 7,200-token chat reaches its first token in 0.19 to 0.21 seconds, against 1.06 to 1.09 seconds without the cache. `--prompt-cache-gib` sizes the kept states (16 GiB by default, inside the CUDA memory budget; 0 turns them off), and prompts shorter than 4,096 tokens keep none. Thanks to @AdrianBinDC, and to @SvangenStudios and @plotarmordev for the port to 1.0.2 and their receipts.
- The routed-expert plan that groups a round's pairs by expert is one shared CUDA module, `cuda/grouped.zig`, for every MoE family to use; Nemotron's kernels, launches and replies are unchanged, and `tf-cuda-test grouped-plan` checks the plan against the Python engine's bytes (#558). Thanks to @jschmied.
- A model-free block-FP8 projection on CUDA (128 x 128 e4m3 blocks with fp32 scales, on the lane matmul's FP8G kernels), equal byte for byte to the Python engine's on a GB10 (`tf-cuda-test fp8-lane`). No model uses it yet, and the CUDA kernel set gains `fp8_lane` (#482). Thanks to @jschmied.
- Experimental, on a GB10: `TF_CUDA_CARVEOUT=1` (or `tensorfold run --carveout`) puts Nemotron's KV caches in the 1.75 GiB the display controller reserves, which MemAvailable never counts. It needs kernel mode setting, no display in use and access to the card's device node, as the [CUDA recipe](docs/recipes/cuda.md) describes, and it stops the load rather than run with less. Off, which is the default, memory, tokens and speed are unchanged (#487). Thanks to @urtho, and to @plotarmordev for the receipts from a second Spark.
- Nemotron on CUDA can pause a long prompt while the machine runs hot: with `TF_HEAT_HIGH` and `TF_HEAT_LOW` set (degrees Celsius), each prompt chunk waits while the hottest thermal zone is above the high band, until it is at or under the low one. The reply's tokens don't change, a cancel ends the wait, and the server logs `heat_wait_s`. Thanks to @Bizuayeu, whose GLM-5.3-Flash measurements on three Sparks (#339) set the design.
- Living Weights learns from Japanese and other text written without spaces, and holds a fact told in another language against prompts in that language, so it doesn't spill into other questions. Text with no usable facts now ends with a message saying so (#570). Thanks to @takanobuouchi-cell.
- `logprobs` and `top_logprobs` (0 to 20) on nonstreamed `/v1/chat/completions` for Nemotron on CUDA. Each reply token's row is its log probability under the model's raw distribution, read on the GPU from the verified target logits, so drafted, `"draft": false`, concurrent and resumed requests give the same rows. With thinking on, the rows cover the answer only, so `logprobs.content[0]` is the first answer token, as @DakotaTexas specified (#449). Streaming, tools, stop strings, structured output and a thinking budget stay refused with `logprobs`, and so do the Metal engines and the Responses and completions routes. On a GB10 every row matched a float64 log-softmax of the same logits within 2e-6, and a 256-token reply with 20 alternatives decoded 7% slower than without (#572). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- Editing, deleting or regenerating a conversation's last turn resumes from a prompt state kept just before the latest user message, on every native engine with a prompt cache, and the reply equals the cache-off reply. The state is kept only when it fits beside the history's, and while its turn lasts it is the last one evicted. On an M3 Ultra, the Qwen3.8-27B's deleted, edited, regenerated and re-edited last turns of a 9,000-token chat took 1.7 s of prompt processing in all against 10.9 s, and on a GB10 replacing a pasted last message resumed at 8,192 tokens instead of 6,144 (#571). Thanks to @otarkhan.
- A two-Mac server whose other rank never answers says what to check, macOS Local Network access included: a server started detached over SSH can't ask for it, and macOS drops its packets. The speed-up guide, recipe and runbook say the same (#567). Thanks to @appljd for finding the cause.
- The CUDA recipe links a user's 1.0.4 receipt on a bare-metal RTX 3090 at 225 W and 350 W (#592). Thanks to @briepala.
- GLM-5.3-Flash runs on one 256 GB M5 Ultra. 1.0.4 refused it because the 4-bit checkpoint with its MTP head, 180.6 GB, is over the one-Mac limit of 70% of RAM. The load now leaves the head on disk when only the head is over, and says so; copy drafts still work. On one M5 Ultra the full model reads 2k, 8k, 16k, 32k and 64k-token prompts at 1,859, 1,990, 1,981, 1,964 and 1,925 tok/s, a served 26.6k-token prompt reaches its first token in 13.7 s, and it decodes prose at 78 tok/s and code edits at 165.
- GLM prompts run more of the model on the M5 tensor units. On two M5 Ultras they read 2.1k, 4k, 8k and 32k tokens at 2,090, 2,079, 2,513 and 2,497 tok/s, against 1,417, 1,559, 1,803 and 1,662 in 1.0.4, and a served 26.9k-token prompt reaches its first token in 10.9 s instead of 16.6. The prompt matmuls unpack 4-bit codes with one OR and sum each row in fp32, which also brings them closer to fp64: 1.1e-6 of the outputs' rms, from 3.8e-3. The routed experts' gate and up run as one gather over the token rows with SwiGLU at its end, and down writes each pair's output in place. KDA's gates and recurrence, the index scores and the sparse attention run on the tensor units, and a chunk's buffers share three regions by layer, 185 KB a row instead of 419, so one Mac takes 8,192-row chunks. A prompt row read in a chunk no longer matches the same row read in a 16-row window bit for bit, so `tf-glm-run` and the server give the same reply to a long prompt only when they cut it in the same places. Served replies still equal fresh ones, because the server cuts a resumed prompt where it cuts a fresh one, and drafted replies still equal plain ones.
- GLM decode on Macs runs attention and index scores on the tensor units, takes the shared expert inside the routed gathers on one Mac, commits plain rounds a round ahead, and sends a drafted round's keeps and drafts to the GPU while the host encodes the next window. On two M5 Ultras code edits decode at 262 tok/s, from 239, and plain decode at 93, from 91.
- GLM keeps lighter prompt states. A kept state holds the KDA states and convolution windows and leaves the MLA prefixes in its slot, valid until that slot writes below them. A 26.6k-token chat's state takes 141 MiB instead of 587, so the full model on one Mac keeps it, and the next turn prefills in 0.15 s instead of 13.8 s with the same reply. `--learn` still keeps whole states.
- GLM loads read the checkpoint around the page cache and wait while free memory is under 3% of RAM. A 160 GB load on a 256 GB M5 Ultra had filled the page cache, run out of free memory and restarted the Mac.
- `tf-moe-bench` and `tf-mm-bench` time routed-expert and prompt matmul kernels from `.metal` files against each other, with each kernel's bits against the first and the matmuls' error against fp64.
- Living Weights' leak check no longer counts an everyday word a reply says in passing as a leak: "If you'd like, I can help" used to undo a round that taught "I like blue." (#590). Thanks to @ZackSample for the report.
- The CUDA engine's quantized projections have one home, `cuda/qlinear.zig`: a weight view per format (MLX affine 4-bit, and #482's block-FP8) with one call for decode rows and one for prompt rows, which refuses a format the family didn't load. Nemotron's 4-bit projections run through it with the same kernels, launches and replies, and `tf-cuda-test fp8-lane` checks the FP8 rows through both calls (#587). Thanks to @jschmied.
- The Qwen3.8-27B loads on M5 Macs running macOS 27 again: under Metal 4.1 its prompt attention and the lane projection's register kernel failed to compile, so the engine stopped with `LibraryLoad` (#573, fixes #579). Thanks to @jkuepker and @ApaApatu.
- A request with a temperature to an engine that decodes greedily only (GLM-5.3-Flash, Flash Next) is refused with a 400 before it is admitted, instead of failing after it waited its turn; when the server itself samples by default, the message says to serve with `--temperature 0` (#582). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- The native reply's `tensorfold.seconds` is its decode time, the span `tokens_per_second` divides, so a long prompt no longer reads as a slow decode; `prefill_seconds` and `time_to_first_token` carry the prompt. The Python 0.6 server's `seconds` stays the whole request (#588). Thanks to @eleqtrizit.
- A completion request whose nested prompt ids (`"prompt": [[...]]`) reach 2^32 or more no longer crashes the server: those ids decode as unknown, as ids past the vocabulary already did (#594). Thanks to @chaog992.
- The repository checks out on Windows: eight server golden files were named after a URL with a query (`get-health?reset_peak=1`) or a `tool_choice` JSON value, and Windows refuses `?`, `"` and `:` in file names. Their names now carry those characters as `%XX`, and `zig build test` fails on any path Windows can't check out.
